Player_Black (to Proxy):

Let me analyze this position carefully:

**Key observations:**
- I'm up significant material (queen vs. minor pieces, an extra pawn)
- My bishop on **d1** is currently hanging — White's king on e1 can capture it with Kxd1, so I must address this immediately
- White's back rank is congested, and several White pawns are weak

**Evaluating my options:**
- **Ne4-g5** (forking the h3-knight and f3-pawn) fails to **Nxg5** — the h3-knight covers g5
- **Ne4-d2** (forking the rook and knight) fails to **Kxd2** — the knight would be undefended
- **Be2** loses the bishop to **Kxe2**

**Best move: Bxc2!** This:
1. Saves the bishop from Kxd1
2. Wins the c2-pawn
3. Attacks the knight on b1 and the b3-pawn, creating ongoing pressure
4. From c2, the bishop also defends my knight on e4 (c2–d3–e4 diagonal), blunting White's only active try (Nc3/Nd2), and keeps the undefended f3-pawn as a future target

**Action: `make_move d1c2`**

Now I can see the full picture. Let me analyze this position:

**Position analysis:**
- I have a knight on **d4** (strongly placed in the center)
- White has a knight on **e4** which is attacked by my **f5 pawn**
- The e4 knight's only defender is the **d3 pawn** (d3 defends e4)
- My queen on e7, both bishops, and both rooks are active

**The key tactic:** Playing **fxe4** (pawn from f5 captures the knight on e4) wins material:
1. If White doesn't recapture, I win a full knight for a pawn
2. If White recaptures with **dxe4**, the trade is: my f5 pawn for White's e4 knight — a clear win of a piece for a pawn, and my powerful knight on d4 remains untouched

I also checked alternatives:
- **Nxf3+** looks tempting (fork on the king), but exf3 simply trades knight for pawn — bad
- **Nxe2** or **Nxc2** both run into the queen on d1 recapturing
- **Nxc2** fork on the rook doesn't work because Qd1 defends c2

The pawn capture is clearly the strongest move — it wins a piece while keeping my dominant knight on d4.

**make_move f5e4**

1. **Knight check on c2** (d4c2): From c2, the knight checks the white king on e1. Critically, the king's escape squares are severely limited — d2 is covered by my e3 pawn, e2/f2/f1 are occupied by white's own pieces. So **Kd1 is forced**!

   - Even if white plays Nf3 to meet the threat, I respond Ne3+! winning the bishop with ongoing threats, keeping my advanced f2 pawn

Compare this to the immediate exf2+ — white simply recaptures with Kxf2 and material stays level. Playing the check first is clearly superior.

My move: **d4c2**

- **Kxd1?** Illegal — my knight on e3 defends d1
- **Kd2?** Illegal — queen attacks d2
- **Ke2?** Illegal — queen attacks e2 (and my f3 pawn also covers e2)

- **Kf2?** Illegal — occupied by White's own pawn
- **Capture the queen on d1?** The rook on b1 is blocked by the bishop on c1; no other piece can reach d1
- **Block the check?** Impossible — it's a contact check adjacent to the king

This is checkmate!

1. **g4e4 (Qxe4)**: My queen escapes the attack AND wins a pawn on e4. Checking if e4 is defended: no white piece attacks e4 (the c4 and e3 pawns don't defend it, no piece reaches it). This also keeps the tension resolved in my favor.

2. Critically, my knight on c2 is attacking the trapped rook on a1 (it has no escape squares — blocked by a2 and the b1 knight). After Qxe4, I threaten Nxa1 winning a full rook, since nothing defends a1.

3. If White responds with Qf3 offering a trade, I simply trade queens (Qxf3 Nxf3) and *then* play Nxa1, staying up a rook plus a pawn.

Other moves like Qe2+ fail to Bxe2 (the f1 bishop defends e2), and Nxa1 immediately would allow Qxg4 winning my undefended queen.

So Qxe4 is clearly the strongest move — it wins a pawn, saves my queen from the diagonal attack, and sets up Nxa1 to win the rook.

make_move g4e4

- Bc1xe3 is impossible — blocked by White's own pawn on d2

- d1 — occupied by White's own queen
- d2 — occupied by White's own pawn
- f1 — occupied by White's own bishop
- d3 — covered by the Queen on e4 (diagonal)

- f2 — covered by the Bishop on e3 (diagonal)
- e1 — covered by the Knight on c2
- e3 — capturing the bishop is illegal (defended twice)

Every square is covered, every capture fails, and no block is possible against a contact check.

This is checkmate! I'll play it.

- **e5e4** — gains tempo by attacking the f3 knight with my pawn, seizes central space, and opens lines for my bishop on c8. Crucially, if White replies Nxh4, the knight gets trapped: I play **g7g5**, and the knight has no escape (g6 loses to hxg6, f5 loses to exf5, f3 loses to exf3, g2 loses to Kxg2) — I win the piece back.

**make_move e5e4**

   - **Kc2** — covered by the bishop on a4
   - **Kd2** — covered by the rook on the d-file
3. So White is **forced** to play **Kb2** — the only legal reply.

Then on my next move I promote with **h2h1=Q**, retaining my rook AND gaining a queen — after which mate follows shortly with the queen + rook + bishop against the bare king.

(This is superior to promoting immediately with h2h1=Q+, since after the forced Kxd2 I would lose my rook — the bishop's defense of d1 makes the rook check the perfect preparation.)

**Action:** `make_move d2d1`
